The following description:

- The Zkr extension adds the SSEED and USEED fields to the mseccfg CSR to control access to the seed CSR from U, S, or HS modes.

- Implementations may implement mseccfg such that [s,u]seed is a read-only constant value 0.

- Software may discover if access to the seed CSR can be enabled in U and S mode by writing a 1 to [s,u]seed and reading back the result.

RULE 2: Software may discover whether access to the seed CSR can be enabled in U and S modes by writing 1 to the SSEED and USEED fields of mseccfg and reading back the results.

The following description:

The direct mseccfg.[s,u]seed option allows one to draw a security boundary around a component in relation to Sensitive Security Parameter (SSP) flows, even if that component is not in M mode.

The direct mseccfg.[s,u]seed option is helpful when implementing trusted enclaves. Such modules can enforce the entire key lifecycle from birth (in the entropy source) to death (zeroization) to occur without the key being passed across the boundary to external code.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The direct mseccfg.[s,u]seed option allows a security boundary to be drawn around a component in relation to Sensitive Security Parameter (SSP) flows even when the component is not in M mode.

RULE 2: The direct mseccfg.[s,u]seed option is helpful when implementing trusted enclaves.

RULE 3: Trusted enclave modules using the direct mseccfg.[s,u]seed option can enforce the entire key lifecycle, from birth in the entropy source to death through zeroization, without passing the key across the security boundary to external code.

The following description:

- CSR number 0x747, privilege MRW, name mseccfg: Machine security configuration register.

- CSR number 0x757, privilege MRW, name mseccfgh: Upper 32 bits of mseccfg, RV32 only.

RULE 5: The mseccfgh register for mseccfg has the privilege designation MRW.

RULE 6: The mseccfgh register contains the upper 32 bits of mseccfg.

RULE 7: The mseccfgh register for mseccfg is present only on RV32.

The following description:

General mseccfg properties

- mseccfg is a 64-bit read/write register that controls security features.
- If any extension that adds a field to mseccfg is implemented, mseccfg exists.
- Otherwise, mseccfg is reserved.

Zkr fields: SSEED and USEED

- The Zkr extension adds the SSEED and USEED fields to the mseccfg CSR to control access to the seed CSR from modes less privileged than M.

- When USEED is 0, access to the seed CSR in U-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
- When USEED is 1, read-write access to the seed CSR from U-mode is allowed; all other types of accesses raise an illegal-instruction exception.
- If Zkr or U-mode is not implemented, USEED is read-only zero.

- When SSEED is 0, access to the seed CSR from S-/HS-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
- When SSEED is 1, read-write access to the seed CSR from S-/HS-mode is allowed; all other types of accesses raise an illegal-instruction exception.
- If Zkr or S-mode is not implemented, SSEED is read-only zero.

- When the H extension is also implemented, access to the seed CSR from an HS-qualified instruction leads to a virtual-instruction exception in VS and VU modes; all other types of accesses raise an illegal-instruction exception.

Entropy Source Access Control

- Mode M, SSEED "-", USEED "-": The seed CSR is always available in machine mode as normal, with a CSR read-write instruction. Attempted read without a write raises an illegal-instruction exception regardless of mode and access control bits.

- Mode U, SSEED "-", USEED 0: Any seed CSR access raises an illegal-instruction exception.

- Mode U, SSEED "-", USEED 1: The seed CSR is accessible as normal. No exception is raised for read-write.

- Mode S/HS, SSEED 0, USEED "-": Any seed CSR access raises an illegal-instruction exception.

- Mode S/HS, SSEED 1, USEED "-": The seed CSR is accessible as normal. No exception is raised for read-write.

- Mode VS/VU, SSEED 0, USEED "-": Any seed CSR access raises an illegal-instruction exception.

- Mode VS/VU, SSEED 1, USEED "-": A read-write seed access raises a virtual-instruction exception, while other access conditions raise an illegal-instruction exception.

Smepmp fields: RLB, MMWP, and MML

- The Smepmp extension adds the RLB, MMWP, and MML fields in mseccfg.

mseccfg.RLB

- mseccfg.RLB (Rule Locking Bypass) is a WARL field that provides a mechanism to temporarily modify Locked PMP rules.
- When mseccfg.RLB is 1, locked PMP rules may be removed or modified and locked PMP rules may be edited.
- When mseccfg.RLB is 0 and pmpcfg.L is 1 in any rule or entry, including disabled entries, mseccfg.RLB remains 0 and any further modifications to mseccfg.RLB are ignored until a PMP reset.
- This feature is intended to be used as a debug mechanism, or as a temporary workaround during the boot process for simplifying software and optimizing the allocation of memory and PMP rules.
- Using this functionality under normal operation, after the boot process is completed, should be avoided since it weakens the protection of M-mode-only rules.
- Vendors who do not need this functionality may hardwire mseccfg.RLB to 0.

mseccfg.MMWP

- mseccfg.MMWP (Machine-Mode Allowlist Policy) is a WARL field.
- mseccfg.MMWP changes the default PMP policy for Machine mode when accessing memory regions that do not have a matching PMP rule.
- mseccfg.MMWP is a sticky bit, meaning that once set it cannot be unset until a PMP reset.
- When mseccfg.MMWP is set, it changes the default PMP policy for M-mode when accessing memory regions that do not have a matching PMP rule to denied instead of ignored.

mseccfg.MML

- mseccfg.MML (Machine Mode Lockdown) is a WARL field.
- The MML bit changes the interpretation of the pmpcfg.L bit.
- mseccfg.MML is a sticky bit, meaning that once set it cannot be unset until a PMP reset.

When mseccfg.MML is set:

1. The meaning of pmpcfg.L changes. Instead of marking a rule as locked and enforced in all modes, pmpcfg.L marks a rule as M-mode-only when set and S/U-mode-only when unset.

2. The formerly reserved encoding of pmpcfg.RW=01 and the encoding pmpcfg.LRWX=1111 encode a Shared-Region.

3. An M-mode-only rule is enforced on Machine mode and denied in Supervisor or User mode.

4. An M-mode-only rule remains locked so that any further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset, unless mseccfg.RLB is set.

5. An S/U-mode-only rule is enforced on Supervisor and User modes and denied on Machine mode.

6. A Shared-Region rule is enforced on all modes, with restrictions depending on the pmpcfg.L and pmpcfg.X bits.

7. When a Shared-Region rule has pmpcfg.L not set, it can be used for sharing data between M-mode and S/U-mode, so it is not executable. M-mode has read/write access to that region. S/U-mode has read access if pmpcfg.X is not set, or read/write access if pmpcfg.X is set.

8. When a Shared-Region rule has pmpcfg.L set, it can be used for sharing code between M-mode and S/U-mode, so it is not writable. Both M-mode and S/U-mode have execute access on the region, and M-mode also has read access if pmpcfg.X is set.

9. When a Shared-Region rule has pmpcfg.L set, the rule remains locked so that any further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset, unless mseccfg.RLB is set.

10. The encoding pmpcfg.LRWX=1111 can be used for sharing data between M-mode and S/U-mode, where both modes only have read-only access to the region.

11. For the encoding pmpcfg.LRWX=1111, the rule remains locked so that any further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset, unless mseccfg.RLB is set.

12. Adding a rule with executable privileges that either is M-mode-only or a locked Shared-Region is not possible, and such pmpcfg writes are ignored, leaving pmpcfg unchanged.

13. The restriction on adding a rule with executable privileges that either is M-mode-only or a locked Shared-Region can be temporarily lifted by setting mseccfg.RLB, for example during the boot process.

14. Executing code with Machine mode privileges is only possible from memory regions with a matching M-mode-only rule or a locked Shared-Region rule with executable privileges.

15. Executing code from a region without a matching rule or with a matching S/U-mode-only rule is denied.

- If mseccfg.MML is not set, the combination pmpcfg.RW=01 remains reserved for future standard use.

PMM field

- If the Smmpm extension is implemented, the PMM field enables or disables pointer masking for M-mode according to the following legal values:

  - PMM=00: Pointer masking is disabled (PMLEN=0).
  - PMM=01: Reserved.
  - PMM=10: Pointer masking is enabled with PMLEN=XLEN-57 (PMLEN=7 on RV64).
  - PMM=11: Pointer masking is enabled with PMLEN=XLEN-48 (PMLEN=16 on RV64).

- PMM is a WARL field.
- If Smmpm is not implemented, PMM is read-only zero.
- For RV32, PMM is read-only zero.
- Smmpm implementations need to satisfy:

  max(largest supported virtual address size,
      largest supported supervisor physical address size)
  <= (XLEN - PMLEN) bits

  to avoid any masking logic on the TLB access path.

MLPE field

- The Zicfilp extension adds the MLPE field in mseccfg.
- When MLPE is 1, the Zicfilp extension is enabled in M-mode.
- When MLPE is 0, the Zicfilp extension is not enabled in M-mode, the hart does not update the ELP state, and the ELP state remains as NO_LP_EXPECTED.
- When MLPE is 0, the LPAD instruction operates as a no-op in M-mode.

mseccfgh

- When XLEN=32 only, mseccfgh is a 32-bit read/write register that aliases bits 63:32 of mseccfg.
- mseccfgh exists when XLEN=32 and mseccfg is implemented.
- mseccfgh does not exist when XLEN=64.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: mseccfg is a 64-bit register.
RULE 2: mseccfg is read/write.
RULE 3: mseccfg controls security features.
RULE 4: mseccfg exists if any implemented extension adds a field to mseccfg.
RULE 5: mseccfg is reserved if no implemented extension adds a field to mseccfg.
RULE 6: The Zkr extension adds the SSEED field to mseccfg.
RULE 7: The Zkr extension adds the USEED field to mseccfg.
RULE 8: The mseccfg.SSEED and mseccfg.USEED fields control access to the seed CSR from modes less privileged than M-mode.
RULE 9: When mseccfg.USEED is 0, any seed CSR access from U-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 10: When mseccfg.USEED is 1, read-write access to the seed CSR from U-mode is allowed without an exception.
RULE 11: When mseccfg.USEED is 1, any seed CSR access type from U-mode other than read-write raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 12: If either Zkr or U-mode is not implemented, mseccfg.USEED is read-only zero.
RULE 13: When mseccfg.SSEED is 0, any seed CSR access from S-mode or HS-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 14: When mseccfg.SSEED is 1, read-write access to the seed CSR from S-mode or HS-mode is allowed without an exception.
RULE 15: When mseccfg.SSEED is 1, any seed CSR access type from S-mode or HS-mode other than read-write raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 16: If either Zkr or S-mode is not implemented, mseccfg.SSEED is read-only zero.
RULE 17: The seed CSR is always accessible as normal from M-mode with a CSR read-write instruction, regardless of the values of mseccfg.SSEED and mseccfg.USEED.
RULE 18: An attempted seed CSR read without a write raises an illegal-instruction exception regardless of the current mode and the values of mseccfg.SSEED and mseccfg.USEED.
RULE 19: When the H extension is implemented and mseccfg.SSEED is 0, any seed CSR access from VS-mode or VU-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 20: When the H extension is implemented and mseccfg.SSEED is 1, a read-write seed CSR access by an HS-qualified instruction in VS-mode or VU-mode raises a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 21: When the H extension is implemented and mseccfg.SSEED is 1, any seed CSR access type from VS-mode or VU-mode other than read-write raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 22: The Smepmp extension adds the RLB field to mseccfg.
RULE 23: The Smepmp extension adds the MMWP field to mseccfg.
RULE 24: The Smepmp extension adds the MML field to mseccfg.
RULE 25: mseccfg.RLB, the Rule Locking Bypass field, is a WARL field.
RULE 26: mseccfg.RLB provides a mechanism for temporarily modifying locked PMP rules.
RULE 27: When mseccfg.RLB is 1, locked PMP rules may be removed, modified, or edited.
RULE 28: When mseccfg.RLB is 0 and pmpcfg.L is 1 in any PMP rule or entry, including a disabled entry, mseccfg.RLB remains 0.
RULE 29: When mseccfg.RLB is 0 and pmpcfg.L is 1 in any PMP rule or entry, including a disabled entry, further modifications to mseccfg.RLB are ignored until a PMP reset.
RULE 30: mseccfg.RLB is intended for use as a debug mechanism.
RULE 31: mseccfg.RLB is intended for use as a temporary workaround during the boot process to simplify software.
RULE 32: mseccfg.RLB is intended for use as a temporary workaround during the boot process to optimize the allocation of memory and PMP rules.
RULE 33: Use of mseccfg.RLB during normal operation after the boot process is complete SHOULD be avoided because it weakens the protection of M-mode-only rules.
RULE 34: mseccfg.MMWP, the Machine-Mode Allowlist Policy field, is a WARL field.
RULE 35: mseccfg.MMWP changes the default PMP policy for M-mode accesses to memory regions without a matching PMP rule.
RULE 36: mseccfg.MMWP is a sticky bit that cannot be unset after it is set until a PMP reset.
RULE 37: When mseccfg.MMWP is set, M-mode access to a memory region without a matching PMP rule is denied instead of ignored.
RULE 38: mseccfg.MML, the Machine Mode Lockdown field, is a WARL field.
RULE 39: mseccfg.MML changes the interpretation of the pmpcfg.L bit.
RULE 40: mseccfg.MML is a sticky bit that cannot be unset after it is set until a PMP reset.
RULE 41: When mseccfg.MML is set, a set pmpcfg.L bit marks a PMP rule as M-mode-only instead of marking the rule as locked and enforced in all modes.
RULE 42: When mseccfg.MML is set, an unset pmpcfg.L bit marks a PMP rule as S/U-mode-only instead of marking the rule as unlocked.
RULE 43: When mseccfg.MML is set, the formerly reserved pmpcfg.RW=01 encoding encodes a Shared-Region rule.
RULE 44: When mseccfg.MML is set, the pmpcfg.LRWX=1111 encoding encodes a Shared-Region rule.
RULE 45: When mseccfg.MML is set, an M-mode-only rule is enforced on M-mode.
RULE 46: When mseccfg.MML is set, an M-mode-only rule is denied in S-mode and U-mode.
RULE 47: When mseccfg.MML is set, an M-mode-only rule remains locked so that modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset unless mseccfg.RLB is set.
RULE 48: When mseccfg.MML is set, an S/U-mode-only rule is enforced on S-mode and U-mode.
RULE 49: When mseccfg.MML is set, an S/U-mode-only rule is denied in M-mode.
RULE 50: When mseccfg.MML is set, a Shared-Region rule is enforced on all modes.
RULE 51: When mseccfg.MML is set, the restrictions applied by a Shared-Region rule depend on the pmpcfg.L and pmpcfg.X bits.
RULE 52: When mseccfg.MML is set and a Shared-Region rule has pmpcfg.L unset, the rule can be used to share data between M-mode and S/U-mode.
RULE 53: When mseccfg.MML is set and a Shared-Region rule has pmpcfg.L unset, the region is not executable.
RULE 54: When mseccfg.MML is set and a Shared-Region rule has pmpcfg.L unset, M-mode has read/write access to the region.
RULE 55: When mseccfg.MML is set and a Shared-Region rule has both pmpcfg.L and pmpcfg.X unset, S-mode and U-mode have read access to the region.
RULE 56: When mseccfg.MML is set, a Shared-Region rule with pmpcfg.L unset and pmpcfg.X set gives S-mode and U-mode read/write access to the region.
RULE 57: When mseccfg.MML is set and a Shared-Region rule has pmpcfg.L set, the rule can be used to share code between M-mode and S/U-mode.
RULE 58: When mseccfg.MML is set and a Shared-Region rule has pmpcfg.L set, the region is not writable.
RULE 59: When mseccfg.MML is set and a Shared-Region rule has pmpcfg.L set, both M-mode and S/U-mode have execute access to the region.
RULE 60: When mseccfg.MML is set and a Shared-Region rule has both pmpcfg.L and pmpcfg.X set, M-mode also has read access to the region.
RULE 61: When mseccfg.MML is set, a Shared-Region rule with pmpcfg.L set remains locked so that modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset unless mseccfg.RLB is set.
RULE 62: When mseccfg.MML is set, the pmpcfg.LRWX=1111 encoding can be used to share data between M-mode and S/U-mode.
RULE 63: When mseccfg.MML is set, a region encoded by pmpcfg.LRWX=1111 provides only read-only access to both M-mode and S/U-mode.
RULE 64: When mseccfg.MML is set, a rule encoded by pmpcfg.LRWX=1111 remains locked so that modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset unless mseccfg.RLB is set.
RULE 65: When mseccfg.MML is set, adding an executable M-mode-only rule or an executable locked Shared-Region rule is not possible.
RULE 66: When mseccfg.MML is set, a pmpcfg write that attempts to add an executable M-mode-only rule or an executable locked Shared-Region rule is ignored and leaves pmpcfg unchanged.
RULE 67: When mseccfg.MML is set, setting mseccfg.RLB can temporarily lift the restriction on adding an executable M-mode-only rule or an executable locked Shared-Region rule, such as during the boot process.
RULE 68: When mseccfg.MML is set, executing code with M-mode privileges is possible only from a memory region with a matching M-mode-only rule or a matching locked Shared-Region rule with executable privileges.
RULE 69: When mseccfg.MML is set, M-mode execution from a memory region without a matching PMP rule is denied.
RULE 70: When mseccfg.MML is set, M-mode execution from a memory region with a matching S/U-mode-only rule is denied.
RULE 71: When mseccfg.MML is not set, the pmpcfg.RW=01 combination remains reserved for future standard use.
RULE 72: If the Smmpm extension is implemented, mseccfg.PMM enables or disables pointer masking for M-mode.
RULE 73: If the Smmpm extension is implemented, mseccfg.PMM=00 disables pointer masking and selects PMLEN=0.
RULE 74: If the Smmpm extension is implemented, mseccfg.PMM=01 is reserved.
RULE 75: If the Smmpm extension is implemented, mseccfg.PMM=10 enables pointer masking with PMLEN=XLEN-57, which is PMLEN=7 on RV64.
RULE 76: If the Smmpm extension is implemented, mseccfg.PMM=11 enables pointer masking with PMLEN=XLEN-48, which is PMLEN=16 on RV64.
RULE 77: mseccfg.PMM is a WARL field.
RULE 78: If the Smmpm extension is not implemented, mseccfg.PMM is read-only zero.
RULE 79: On RV32, mseccfg.PMM is read-only zero.
RULE 80: An Smmpm implementation containing mseccfg.PMM needs to satisfy max(largest supported virtual address size, largest supported supervisor physical address size) <= (XLEN - PMLEN) bits to avoid any masking logic on the TLB access path.
RULE 81: The Zicfilp extension adds the MLPE field to mseccfg.
RULE 82: When mseccfg.MLPE is 1, the Zicfilp extension is enabled in M-mode.
RULE 83: When mseccfg.MLPE is 0, the Zicfilp extension is not enabled in M-mode.
RULE 84: When mseccfg.MLPE is 0, the hart does not update the ELP state.
RULE 85: When mseccfg.MLPE is 0, the ELP state remains NO_LP_EXPECTED.
RULE 86: When mseccfg.MLPE is 0, the LPAD instruction operates as a no-op in M-mode.
RULE 87: When XLEN=32, mseccfgh is a 32-bit read/write register that aliases bits 63:32 of mseccfg.
RULE 88: mseccfgh exists when XLEN=32 and mseccfg is implemented.
RULE 89: When XLEN=64, mseccfgh, the high-half alias register for mseccfg, does not exist.
======================================================= END - 4 ========================================================

- If the Zicfilp extension is implemented, the mseccfg.MLPE field is reset to 0.

RULE 4: The system must not allow the mseccfg.USEED and mseccfg.SSEED fields to be in an undefined state after reset.

The following description:

- To address the threat model outlined in Section 6.1, the extension introduces the RLB, MMWP, and MML fields in the mseccfg CSR and their associated rules.

- When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=0, W=0, X=0:
  - MMode: Inaccessible region (Access Exception).
  - S/U Mode: Inaccessible region (Access Exception).

- When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=0, W=0, X=1:
  - MMode: Access Exception.
  - S/U Mode: Execute-only region.

- When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=0, W=1, X=0:
  - MMode: Shared data region: Read/write on Mmode, read-only on S/U mode.
  - S/U Mode: Shared data region: Read/write on Mmode, read-only on S/U mode.

- When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=0, W=1, X=1:
  - MMode: Shared data region: Read/write for both M and S/U mode.
  - S/U Mode: Shared data region: Read/write for both M and S/U mode.

- When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=1, W=0, X=0:
  - MMode: Access Exception.
  - S/U Mode: Read-only region.

- When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=1, W=0, X=1:
  - MMode: Access Exception.
  - S/U Mode: Read/Execute region.

- When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=1, W=1, X=0:
  - MMode: Access Exception.
  - S/U Mode: Read/Write region.

- When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=1, W=1, X=1:
  - MMode: Access Exception.
  - S/U Mode: Read/Write/Execute region.

- When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=0, W=0, X=0:
  - MMode: Locked inaccessible region* (Access Exception).
  - S/U Mode: Locked inaccessible region* (Access Exception).

- When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=0, W=0, X=1:
  - MMode: Locked Execute-only region*.
  - S/U Mode: Access Exception.

- When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=0, W=1, X=0:
  - MMode: Locked Shared code region: Execute only on both M and S/U mode.*
  - S/U Mode: Locked Shared code region: Execute only on both M and S/U mode.*

- When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=0, W=1, X=1:
  - MMode: Locked Shared code region: Execute only on S/U mode, read/execute on Mmode.*
  - S/U Mode: Locked Shared code region: Execute only on S/U mode, read/execute on Mmode.*

- When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=1, W=0, X=0:
  - MMode: Locked Read-only region*.
  - S/U Mode: Access Exception.

- When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=1, W=0, X=1:
  - MMode: Locked Read/Execute region*.
  - S/U Mode: Access Exception.

- When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=1, W=1, X=0:
  - MMode: Locked Read/Write region*.
  - S/U Mode: Access Exception.

- When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=1, W=1, X=1:
  - MMode: Locked Shared data region: Read only on both M and S/U mode.*
  - S/U Mode: Locked Shared data region: Read only on both M and S/U mode.*
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The extension introduces the RLB, MMWP, and MML fields in the mseccfg CSR.

RULE 2: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=0, W=0, and X=0, the region is inaccessible in M-mode and an M-mode access results in an access exception.

RULE 3: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=0, W=0, and X=0, the region is inaccessible in S/U-mode and an S/U-mode access results in an access exception.

RULE 4: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=0, W=0, and X=1, an M-mode access results in an access exception.

RULE 5: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=0, W=0, and X=1, the region is execute-only in S/U-mode.

RULE 6: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=0, W=1, and X=0, the region is a shared data region with read/write access in M-mode.

RULE 7: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=0, W=1, and X=0, the region is a shared data region with read-only access in S/U-mode.

RULE 8: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=0, W=1, and X=1, the region is a shared data region with read/write access in M-mode.

RULE 9: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=0, W=1, and X=1, the region is a shared data region with read/write access in S/U-mode.

RULE 10: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=1, W=0, and X=0, an M-mode access results in an access exception.

RULE 11: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=1, W=0, and X=0, the region is read-only in S/U-mode.

RULE 12: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=1, W=0, and X=1, an M-mode access results in an access exception.

RULE 13: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=1, W=0, and X=1, the region permits read and execute access in S/U-mode.

RULE 14: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=1, W=1, and X=0, an M-mode access results in an access exception.

RULE 15: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=1, W=1, and X=0, the region permits read and write access in S/U-mode.

RULE 16: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=1, W=1, and X=1, an M-mode access results in an access exception.

RULE 17: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=0, R=1, W=1, and X=1, the region permits read, write, and execute access in S/U-mode.

RULE 18: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=0, W=0, and X=0, the region is locked and inaccessible in M-mode and an M-mode access results in an access exception.

RULE 19: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=0, W=0, and X=0, the region is locked and inaccessible in S/U-mode and an S/U-mode access results in an access exception.

RULE 20: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=0, W=0, and X=1, the region is a locked execute-only region in M-mode.

RULE 21: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=0, W=0, and X=1, an S/U-mode access results in an access exception.

RULE 22: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=0, W=1, and X=0, the region is a locked shared code region with execute-only access in M-mode.

RULE 23: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=0, W=1, and X=0, the region is a locked shared code region with execute-only access in S/U-mode.

RULE 24: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=0, W=1, and X=1, the region is a locked shared code region with read and execute access in M-mode.

RULE 25: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=0, W=1, and X=1, the region is a locked shared code region with execute-only access in S/U-mode.

RULE 26: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=1, W=0, and X=0, the region is a locked read-only region in M-mode.

RULE 27: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=1, W=0, and X=0, an S/U-mode access results in an access exception.

RULE 28: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=1, W=0, and X=1, the region is a locked read/execute region in M-mode.

RULE 29: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=1, W=0, and X=1, an S/U-mode access results in an access exception.

RULE 30: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=1, W=1, and X=0, the region is a locked read/write region in M-mode.

RULE 31: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=1, W=1, and X=0, an S/U-mode access results in an access exception.

RULE 32: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=1, W=1, and X=1, the region is a locked shared data region with read-only access in M-mode.

RULE 33: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg bits are L=1, R=1, W=1, and X=1, the region is a locked shared data region with read-only access in S/U-mode.

The following description:

1. A vector context status field, VS, is added to mstatus[10:9] and shadowed in sstatus[10:9]. It is defined analogously to the floating-point context status field, FS.

2. Attempts to execute any vector instruction, or to access the vector CSRs, raise an illegal-instruction exception when mstatus.VS is set to Off.

3. When mstatus.VS is set to Initial or Clean, executing any instruction that changes vector state, including the vector CSRs, will change mstatus.VS to Dirty.

4. Implementations may change mstatus.VS from Initial or Clean to Dirty at any time, even when there is no change in vector state.

5. Accurate setting of mstatus.VS is an optimization. Software will typically use VS to reduce context-swap overhead.

6. If mstatus.VS is Dirty, mstatus.SD is 1.

7. Otherwise, mstatus.SD is set in accordance with existing specifications.

8. Analogous to the way in which the floating-point unit is handled, the mstatus.VS field may exist even if misa.V is clear.

9. Allowing mstatus.VS to exist when misa.V is clear enables vector emulation and simplifies handling of mstatus.VS in systems with writable misa.V.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The mstatus.VS vector context status field occupies mstatus[10:9].
RULE 2: The mstatus.VS field is shadowed in sstatus[10:9].
RULE 3: The mstatus.VS field is defined analogously to the mstatus.FS floating-point context status field.
RULE 4: An attempt to execute any vector instruction raises an illegal-instruction exception when mstatus.VS is Off.
RULE 5: An attempt to access any vector CSR raises an illegal-instruction exception when mstatus.VS is Off.
RULE 6: When mstatus.VS is Initial or Clean, executing any instruction that changes vector state, including any vector CSR, changes mstatus.VS to Dirty.
RULE 7: Accurate setting of mstatus.VS is an optimization.
RULE 8: Software will typically use mstatus.VS to reduce context-swap overhead.
RULE 9: If mstatus.VS is Dirty, mstatus.SD is 1.
RULE 10: When mstatus.VS is not Dirty, mstatus.SD retains its previously specified semantics.
RULE 11: The mstatus.VS field may exist even when misa.V is clear, analogously to the handling of the floating-point unit.
RULE 12: Allowing mstatus.VS to exist when misa.V is clear enables vector emulation.
RULE 13: Allowing mstatus.VS to exist when misa.V is clear simplifies handling of mstatus.VS in systems with writable misa.V.

The following description:

- When the hypervisor extension is present, a vector context status field, VS, is added to vsstatus[10:9]. It is defined analogously to the floating-point context status field, FS.

- When V=1, both vsstatus.VS and mstatus.VS are in effect: attempts to execute any vector instruction, or to access the vector CSRs, raise an illegal-instruction exception when either field is set to Off.

- When V=1 and neither vsstatus.VS nor mstatus.VS is set to Off, executing any instruction that changes vector state, including the vector CSRs, will change both mstatus.VS and vsstatus.VS to Dirty.

- Implementations may change mstatus.VS or vsstatus.VS from Initial or Clean to Dirty at any time, even when there is no change in vector state.

- If mstatus.VS is Dirty, mstatus.SD is 1.

- Otherwise, mstatus.SD is set in accordance with existing specifications.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: When V=1, both mstatus.VS and vsstatus.VS are in effect.

RULE 2: When V=1, an attempt to execute any vector instruction raises an illegal-instruction exception if either mstatus.VS or vsstatus.VS is Off.

RULE 3: When V=1, an attempt to access any vector CSR raises an illegal-instruction exception if either mstatus.VS or vsstatus.VS is Off.

RULE 4: When V=1 and neither mstatus.VS nor vsstatus.VS is Off, executing any instruction that changes vector state, including the vector CSRs, changes both mstatus.VS and vsstatus.VS to Dirty.

RULE 5: If mstatus.VS is Dirty, mstatus.SD is 1.

RULE 6: If mstatus.VS is not Dirty, mstatus.SD is set in accordance with existing specifications.

The following description:

- If the floating-point unit status field mstatus.FS is Off, then any attempt to execute a vector floating-point instruction will raise an illegal-instruction exception.

- Any vector floating-point instruction that modifies any floating-point extension state (i.e., floating-point CSRs or f registers) must set mstatus.FS to Dirty.

The following description:

- The mstatus register provides the global interrupt-enable bits MIE and SIE for M-mode and S-mode, respectively. These bits are primarily used to guarantee atomicity with respect to interrupt handlers in the current privilege mode.

- The global xIE bits are located in the low-order bits of mstatus, allowing them to be atomically set or cleared with a single CSR instruction.

- When a hart is executing in privilege mode x:
  - When xIE=1, interrupts are globally enabled.
  - When xIE=0, interrupts are globally disabled.

- When a hart is executing in privilege mode x and w < x, interrupts for lower-privilege modes w are always globally disabled regardless of the setting of any global wIE bit for the lower-privilege mode.

- When a hart is executing in privilege mode x and y > x, interrupts for higher-privilege modes y are always globally enabled regardless of the setting of the global yIE bit for the higher-privilege mode.

- If supervisor mode is not implemented, SIE and SPIE are read-only 0.

- To support nested traps, each privilege mode x that can respond to interrupts has a two-level stack of interrupt-enable bits and privilege modes:
  - xPIE holds the value of the interrupt-enable bit active prior to the trap.
  - xPP holds the previous privilege mode.

- The xPP fields can only hold privilege modes up to x. Consequently, MPP is two bits wide and SPP is one bit wide.

- When a trap is taken from privilege mode y into privilege mode x:
  - xPIE is set to the value of xIE.
  - xIE is set to 0.
  - xPP is set to y.

- For lower privilege modes, any trap, synchronous or asynchronous, is usually taken at a higher privilege mode with interrupts disabled upon entry.

- An MRET instruction is used to return from a trap in M-mode. An SRET instruction is used to return from a trap in S-mode.

- When executing an xRET instruction, supposing xPP holds the value y:
  - xIE is set to xPIE.
  - The privilege mode is changed to y.
  - xPIE is set to 1.
  - xPP is set to the least-privileged supported mode: U if U-mode is implemented, else M.

- When executing an xRET instruction, if y != M, xRET also sets MPRV=0.

- Setting xPP to the least-privileged supported mode on an xRET helps identify software bugs in the management of the two-level privilege-mode stack.

- The xPP fields are WARL fields that can hold only privilege mode x and any implemented privilege mode lower than x.

- If privilege mode x is not implemented, xPP must be read-only 0.

- M-mode software can determine whether a privilege mode is implemented by writing that mode to MPP and then reading it back.

- If the machine provides only U and M modes, only a single hardware storage bit is required to represent either 00 or 11 in MPP.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The mstatus register provides the global interrupt-enable field MIE for M-mode and the global interrupt-enable field SIE for S-mode.

RULE 2: The MIE and SIE fields in mstatus are primarily used to guarantee atomicity with respect to interrupt handlers in the current privilege mode.

RULE 3: The global xIE fields are located in the low-order bits of mstatus.

RULE 4: The low-order placement of the global xIE fields in mstatus allows them to be atomically set or cleared with a single CSR instruction.

RULE 5: When a hart executes in privilege mode x and the corresponding xIE field in mstatus is 1, interrupts are globally enabled.

RULE 6: When a hart executes in privilege mode x and the corresponding xIE field in mstatus is 0, interrupts are globally disabled.

RULE 7: When a hart executes in privilege mode x, interrupts for every lower privilege mode w where w < x are always globally disabled regardless of the corresponding global wIE field in mstatus.

RULE 8: When a hart executes in privilege mode x, interrupts for every higher privilege mode y where y > x are always globally enabled regardless of the corresponding global yIE field in mstatus.

RULE 9: If supervisor mode is not implemented, the SIE field in mstatus is read-only 0.

RULE 10: If supervisor mode is not implemented, the SPIE field in mstatus is read-only 0.

RULE 11: For each privilege mode x that can respond to interrupts, mstatus provides a two-level stack of interrupt-enable bits and privilege modes to support nested traps.

RULE 12: The xPIE field in mstatus holds the value of the interrupt-enable bit that was active before a trap.

RULE 13: The xPP field in mstatus holds the previous privilege mode.

RULE 14: Each xPP field in mstatus can hold only privilege modes that are no more privileged than privilege mode x.

RULE 15: The MPP field in mstatus is two bits wide.

RULE 16: The SPP field in mstatus is one bit wide.

RULE 17: When a trap is taken from privilege mode y into privilege mode x, the xPIE field in mstatus is set to the value of the xIE field in mstatus.

RULE 18: When a trap is taken from privilege mode y into privilege mode x, the xIE field in mstatus is set to 0.

RULE 19: When a trap is taken from privilege mode y into privilege mode x, the xPP field in mstatus is set to y.

RULE 20: For a lower privilege mode, the mstatus interrupt-enable state is usually disabled upon entry to any synchronous or asynchronous trap because the trap is usually taken at a higher privilege mode.

RULE 21: For mstatus trap-return processing, an MRET instruction is used to return from a trap in M-mode.

RULE 22: For mstatus trap-return processing, an SRET instruction is used to return from a trap in S-mode.

RULE 23: When an xRET instruction executes with the xPP field in mstatus holding privilege mode y, the xIE field in mstatus is set to the value of the xPIE field in mstatus.

RULE 24: When an xRET instruction executes with the xPP field in mstatus holding privilege mode y, the privilege mode is changed to y.

RULE 25: When an xRET instruction executes with the xPP field in mstatus holding privilege mode y, the xPIE field in mstatus is set to 1.

RULE 26: When an xRET instruction executes with the xPP field in mstatus holding privilege mode y, the xPP field in mstatus is set to U-mode if U-mode is implemented, or to M-mode otherwise.

RULE 27: When an xRET instruction executes with the xPP field in mstatus holding privilege mode y and y != M, the MPRV field in mstatus is also set to 0.

RULE 28: Setting the xPP field in mstatus to the least-privileged supported mode during an xRET instruction helps identify software bugs in the management of the two-level privilege-mode stack.

RULE 29: Each xPP field in mstatus is a WARL field that can hold only privilege mode x and any implemented privilege mode lower than x.

RULE 30: If privilege mode x is not implemented, the corresponding xPP field in mstatus MUST be read-only 0.

RULE 31: M-mode software can determine whether a privilege mode is implemented by writing that privilege mode to the MPP field in mstatus and then reading the MPP field back.

RULE 32: If a machine provides only U-mode and M-mode, only one hardware storage bit is required for the MPP field in mstatus to represent either 00 or 11.
======================================================= END - 16 =======================================================

The following description:

- The M-mode-disable-trap (MDT) bit is a WARL field introduced by the Smdbltrp extension. For RV32, the MDT bit is in mstatush, and the MIE bit is in mstatus.

- Upon reset, the MDT field is set to 1.

- When the MDT bit is set to 1 by an explicit CSR write, the MIE (Machine Interrupt Enable) bit is cleared to 0.

- For RV64, when the MDT bit is set to 1 by an explicit CSR write, the MIE bit is cleared to 0 regardless of the value written, if any, to the MIE bit by the same write.

- The MIE bit can only be set to 1 by an explicit CSR write if the MDT bit is already 0 or, for RV64, is being set to 0 by the same write.

- When a trap is to be taken into M-mode, if the MDT bit is currently 0, it is set to 1, and the trap is delivered as expected.

- When a trap is to be taken into M-mode, if MDT is already set to 1, the trap is an unexpected trap.

- When the Smrnmi extension is implemented, a trap caused by an RNMI is not considered an unexpected trap irrespective of the state of the MDT bit.

- A trap caused by an RNMI does not set the MDT bit.

- A trap that occurs when executing in M-mode with mnstatus.NMIE set to 0 is an unexpected trap.

- In the event of an unexpected trap, when the Smrnmi extension is implemented and mnstatus.NMIE is 1, the hart traps to the RNMI handler.

- In the event of an unexpected trap, when the Smrnmi extension is not implemented, or if the Smrnmi extension is implemented and mnstatus.NMIE is 0, the hart enters a critical-error state without updating any architectural state. This state involves ceasing execution, disabling all interrupts, including NMIs, and asserting a critical-error signal to the platform.

- The MRET instruction, when executed in M-mode, sets the MDT bit to 0.

- The SRET instruction, when executed in M-mode, sets the MDT bit to 0.

- The MNRET instruction, provided by the Smrnmi extension, sets the MDT bit to 0 if the new privilege mode is not M.

The following description:

1. For RV64 harts, the mstatus SXL and UXL fields are WARL fields that control the value of XLEN for S-mode and U-mode, respectively. The encoding of these fields is the same as the MXL field of misa. The effective XLEN in S-mode and U-mode are termed SXLEN and UXLEN, respectively.

2. When MXLEN=32, the mstatus SXL and UXL fields do not exist, and SXLEN=32 and UXLEN=32.

3. When MXLEN=64, if S-mode is not supported, then the mstatus SXL field is read-only zero.

4. When MXLEN=64, otherwise, the mstatus SXL field is a WARL field that encodes the current value of SXLEN. In particular, an implementation may make SXL be a read-only field whose value always ensures that SXLEN=MXLEN.

5. When MXLEN=64, if U-mode is not supported, then the mstatus UXL field is read-only zero.

6. When MXLEN=64, otherwise, the mstatus UXL field is a WARL field that encodes the current value of UXLEN. In particular, an implementation may make UXL be a read-only field whose value always ensures that UXLEN=MXLEN or UXLEN=SXLEN.

7. If S-mode is implemented, the set of legal values that the mstatus UXL field may assume excludes those that would cause UXLEN to be greater than SXLEN.

8. Whenever XLEN in any mode is set to a value less than the widest supported XLEN:
   - All operations must ignore source operand register bits above the configured XLEN.
   - All operations must sign-extend results to fill the entire widest supported XLEN in the destination register.
   - pc bits above XLEN are ignored.
   - When the pc is written, it is sign-extended to fill the widest supported XLEN.

9. Operations are required always to fill the entire underlying hardware registers with defined values to avoid implementation-defined behavior.

10. The architecture imposes no checks that lower-privilege modes have XLEN settings less than or equal to the next-higher privilege mode. Machine operation is well-defined even when lower-privilege modes have such settings.

11. Some HINT instructions are encoded as integer computational instructions that overwrite their destination register with its current value, e.g., c.addi x8, 0. When such a HINT is executed with XLEN < MXLEN and bits MXLEN..XLEN of the destination register not all equal to bit XLEN-1, it is implementation-defined whether bits MXLEN..XLEN of the destination register:
   - are unchanged; or
   - are overwritten with copies of bit XLEN-1.

12. The HINT definition allows implementations to elide register write-back for some HINTs, while allowing them to execute other HINTs in the same manner as other integer computational instructions. The implementation choice is observable only by privilege modes with an XLEN setting greater than the current XLEN; it is invisible to the current privilege mode.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: For RV64 harts, the mstatus SXL field is a WARL field.

RULE 2: For RV64 harts, the mstatus SXL field controls the value of XLEN for S-mode.

RULE 3: For RV64 harts, the mstatus UXL field is a WARL field.

RULE 4: For RV64 harts, the mstatus UXL field controls the value of XLEN for U-mode.

RULE 5: The encoding of the mstatus SXL field is the same as the encoding of the MXL field of misa.

RULE 6: The encoding of the mstatus UXL field is the same as the encoding of the MXL field of misa.

RULE 7: The effective S-mode XLEN controlled by the mstatus SXL field is termed SXLEN.

RULE 8: The effective U-mode XLEN controlled by the mstatus UXL field is termed UXLEN.

RULE 9: When MXLEN=32, the mstatus SXL field does not exist.

RULE 10: When MXLEN=32, the mstatus UXL field does not exist.

RULE 11: When MXLEN=32, SXLEN associated with mstatus is 32.

RULE 12: When MXLEN=32, UXLEN associated with mstatus is 32.

RULE 13: When MXLEN=64 and S-mode is not supported, the mstatus SXL field is read-only zero.

RULE 14: When MXLEN=64 and S-mode is supported, the mstatus SXL field is a WARL field that encodes the current value of SXLEN.

RULE 15: When MXLEN=64 and U-mode is not supported, the mstatus UXL field is read-only zero.

RULE 16: When MXLEN=64 and U-mode is supported, the mstatus UXL field is a WARL field that encodes the current value of UXLEN.

RULE 17: If S-mode is implemented, the legal values of the mstatus UXL field exclude values that would cause UXLEN to be greater than SXLEN.

RULE 18: Whenever an S-mode or U-mode XLEN controlled by mstatus is set below the widest supported XLEN, all operations must ignore source operand register bits above the configured XLEN.

RULE 19: Whenever an S-mode or U-mode XLEN controlled by mstatus is set below the widest supported XLEN, all operations must sign-extend results to fill the entire widest supported XLEN in the destination register.

RULE 20: Whenever an S-mode or U-mode XLEN controlled by mstatus is set below the widest supported XLEN, pc bits above the configured XLEN are ignored.

RULE 21: Whenever an S-mode or U-mode XLEN controlled by mstatus is set below the widest supported XLEN, a written pc is sign-extended to fill the widest supported XLEN.

RULE 22: Operations performed under an S-mode or U-mode XLEN setting controlled by mstatus are required always to fill the entire underlying hardware registers with defined values to avoid implementation-defined behavior.

RULE 23: The architecture imposes no checks requiring a lower-privilege-mode XLEN setting controlled by mstatus to be less than or equal to the XLEN setting of the next-higher privilege mode.

RULE 24: Machine operation is well-defined when a lower-privilege-mode XLEN setting controlled by mstatus is greater than the XLEN setting of the next-higher privilege mode.

RULE 25: When a HINT encoded as an integer computational instruction that overwrites its destination register with its current value, such as c.addi x8, 0, executes with an XLEN controlled by mstatus that is less than MXLEN and bits MXLEN..XLEN of the destination register are not all equal to bit XLEN-1, it is implementation-defined whether those destination-register bits remain unchanged or are overwritten with copies of bit XLEN-1.

RULE 26: For HINT execution under a current XLEN controlled by mstatus, the HINT definition permits register write-back to be elided for some HINTs while permitting other HINTs to execute in the same manner as other integer computational instructions.

RULE 27: The choice between eliding HINT register write-back and executing a HINT like another integer computational instruction under a current XLEN controlled by mstatus is observable only by privilege modes whose XLEN setting is greater than the current XLEN.

RULE 28: The choice between eliding HINT register write-back and executing a HINT like another integer computational instruction under a current XLEN controlled by mstatus is invisible to the current privilege mode.

The following description:

MPRV:

- The MPRV (Modify PRiVilege) bit modifies the effective privilege mode, i.e., the privilege level at which explicit memory accesses execute.

- When MPRV=0, explicit memory accesses behave as normal, using the translation and protection mechanisms of the current privilege mode.

- When MPRV=1, load and store memory addresses are translated and protected, and endianness is applied, as though the current privilege mode were set to MPP.

- Instruction address-translation and protection are unaffected by the setting of MPRV.

- If U-mode is not supported, MPRV is read-only 0.

- An MRET or SRET instruction that changes the privilege mode to a mode less privileged than M also sets MPRV=0.

- When MPRV=1, load and store memory addresses are treated as though the current XLEN were set to MPP's XLEN, following the rules in Section 3.1.6.3. The current privilege mode and the privilege mode specified by MPP might have different XLEN settings.

- When MPRV=1 and MPP=S, SUM is in effect, although SUM is ordinarily ignored when not executing in S-mode.

- MPRV obviates the need for M-mode routines that emulate missing hardware features, such as misaligned loads and stores, to perform address translation in software.

MXR:

- The MXR (Make eXecutable Readable) bit modifies the privilege with which loads access virtual memory.

- When MXR=0, only loads from pages marked readable (R=1) will succeed.

- When MXR=1, loads from pages marked either readable or executable (R=1 or X=1) will succeed.

- When page-based virtual memory is not in effect, MXR has no effect.

- If S-mode is not supported, MXR is read-only 0.

- MXR allows instruction words to be loaded from pages marked execute-only.

SUM:

- The SUM (permit Supervisor User Memory access) bit modifies the privilege with which S-mode loads and stores access virtual memory.

- When SUM=0, S-mode memory accesses to pages that are accessible by U-mode (U=1) will fault.

- When SUM=1, these accesses are permitted.

- When page-based virtual memory is not in effect, SUM has no effect.

- SUM is ordinarily ignored when not executing in S-mode.

- When MPRV=1 and MPP=S, SUM is in effect.

- If S-mode is not supported or if satp.MODE is read-only 0, SUM is read-only 0.

MXR and SUM permission scope:

- The MXR and SUM mechanisms only affect the interpretation of permissions encoded in page-table entries.

- The MXR and SUM mechanisms have no impact on whether access-fault exceptions are raised due to PMAs or PMP.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The MPRV (Modify PRiVilege) bit in mstatus modifies the effective privilege mode, which is the privilege level at which explicit memory accesses execute.

RULE 2: When the MPRV bit in mstatus is 0, explicit memory accesses behave normally by using the translation and protection mechanisms of the current privilege mode.

RULE 3: When the MPRV bit in mstatus is 1, load and store memory addresses are translated and protected as though the current privilege mode were set to the mode specified by the MPP field in mstatus.

RULE 4: When the MPRV bit in mstatus is 1, endianness is applied to loads and stores as though the current privilege mode were set to the mode specified by the MPP field in mstatus.

RULE 5: Instruction address translation is unaffected by the setting of the MPRV bit in mstatus.

RULE 6: Instruction protection is unaffected by the setting of the MPRV bit in mstatus.

RULE 7: If U-mode is not supported, the MPRV bit in mstatus is read-only 0.

RULE 8: An MRET instruction that changes the privilege mode to a mode less privileged than M also sets the MPRV bit in mstatus to 0.

RULE 9: An SRET instruction that changes the privilege mode to a mode less privileged than M also sets the MPRV bit in mstatus to 0.

RULE 10: When the MPRV bit in mstatus is 1, load and store memory addresses are treated as though the current XLEN were set to the XLEN of the privilege mode specified by the MPP field in mstatus.

RULE 11: The current privilege mode and the privilege mode specified by the MPP field in mstatus might have different XLEN settings.

RULE 12: When the MPRV bit in mstatus is 1 and the MPP field in mstatus specifies S-mode, the SUM bit in mstatus is in effect.

RULE 13: The MPRV bit in mstatus obviates the need for M-mode routines that emulate missing hardware features, such as misaligned loads and stores, to perform address translation in software.

RULE 14: The MXR (Make eXecutable Readable) bit in mstatus modifies the privilege with which loads access virtual memory.

RULE 15: When the MXR bit in mstatus is 0, only loads from pages marked readable with R=1 will succeed.

RULE 16: When the MXR bit in mstatus is 1, loads from pages marked readable or executable with R=1 or X=1 will succeed.

RULE 17: When page-based virtual memory is not in effect, the MXR bit in mstatus has no effect.

RULE 18: If S-mode is not supported, the MXR bit in mstatus is read-only 0.

RULE 19: The MXR bit in mstatus allows instruction words to be loaded from pages marked execute-only.

RULE 20: The SUM (permit Supervisor User Memory access) bit in mstatus modifies the privilege with which S-mode loads and stores access virtual memory.

RULE 21: When the SUM bit in mstatus is 0, S-mode loads and stores to pages accessible by U-mode with U=1 will fault.

RULE 22: When the SUM bit in mstatus is 1, S-mode loads and stores to pages accessible by U-mode with U=1 are permitted.

RULE 23: When page-based virtual memory is not in effect, the SUM bit in mstatus has no effect.

RULE 24: The SUM bit in mstatus is ordinarily ignored when execution is not in S-mode.

RULE 25: If S-mode is not supported, the SUM bit in mstatus is read-only 0.

RULE 26: If satp.MODE is read-only 0, the SUM bit in mstatus is read-only 0.

RULE 27: The MXR and SUM mechanisms in mstatus affect only the interpretation of permissions encoded in page-table entries.

RULE 28: The MXR and SUM mechanisms in mstatus have no impact on whether access-fault exceptions are raised due to physical memory attributes or physical memory protection.
======================================================= END - 19 =======================================================

The following description:

1. The MBE, SBE, and UBE bits in mstatus and mstatush are WARL fields that control the endianness of memory accesses other than instruction fetches. Instruction fetches are always little-endian.

2. MBE controls whether non-instruction-fetch memory accesses made from M-mode, assuming mstatus.MPRV=0, are little-endian when MBE=0 or big-endian when MBE=1.

3. If S-mode is not supported, SBE is read-only 0.

4. If S-mode is supported, SBE controls whether explicit load and store memory accesses made from S-mode are little-endian when SBE=0 or big-endian when SBE=1.

5. If U-mode is not supported, UBE is read-only 0.

6. If U-mode is supported, UBE controls whether explicit load and store memory accesses made from U-mode are little-endian when UBE=0 or big-endian when UBE=1.

7. For implicit accesses to supervisor-level memory-management data structures, such as page tables, endianness is always controlled by SBE.

8. Changing SBE alters the implementation's interpretation of supervisor-level memory-management data structures. If any such data structures remain in use across a change to SBE, M-mode software must follow such a change to SBE by executing an SFENCE.VMA instruction with rs1=x0 and rs2=x0.

9. When SBE is changed at runtime on world switches and neither the old nor new memory-management data structure is reinterpreted in a different endianness, no additional SFENCE.VMA is necessary beyond what would ordinarily be required for a world switch.

10. If S-mode is supported, an implementation may make SBE be a read-only copy of MBE.

11. If U-mode is supported, an implementation may make UBE be a read-only copy of either MBE or SBE.

12. An implementation supports only little-endian memory accesses if MBE, SBE, and UBE are all read-only 0.

13. An implementation supports only big-endian memory accesses, aside from instruction fetches, if MBE is read-only 1 and SBE and UBE are each read-only 1 when S-mode and U-mode are supported.

14. The correspondence between addresses and byte locations is fixed and is not affected by any endianness mode. The applicable endianness mode determines the order of mapping between memory bytes and a multibyte quantity, such as a halfword or word.

15. RISC-V instructions are uniformly little-endian and are decoupled from the current endianness settings.

16. In big-endian mode, software that encodes or decodes machine instructions must account for explicit loads and stores having endianness opposite that of instructions, for example by swapping byte order after loads and before stores.

RULE 3: Instruction fetches are always little-endian regardless of the MBE, SBE, and UBE settings in mstatus and mstatush.

RULE 4: For non-instruction-fetch memory accesses made from M-mode while mstatus.MPRV=0, the MBE field in mstatus or mstatush selects little-endian operation when MBE=0 or big-endian operation when MBE=1.

RULE 5: If S-mode is not supported, the SBE field in mstatus or mstatush is read-only 0.

RULE 6: If S-mode is supported, the SBE field in mstatus or mstatush selects little-endian operation when SBE=0 or big-endian operation when SBE=1 for explicit load and store memory accesses made from S-mode.

RULE 7: If U-mode is not supported, the UBE field in mstatus or mstatush is read-only 0.

RULE 8: If U-mode is supported, the UBE field in mstatus or mstatush selects little-endian operation when UBE=0 or big-endian operation when UBE=1 for explicit load and store memory accesses made from U-mode.

RULE 9: The SBE field in mstatus or mstatush always controls the endianness of implicit accesses to supervisor-level memory-management data structures, such as page tables.

RULE 10: Changing the SBE field in mstatus or mstatush alters the implementation's interpretation of supervisor-level memory-management data structures.

RULE 12: When the SBE field in mstatus or mstatush is changed at runtime during a world switch and neither the old nor the new memory-management data structure is reinterpreted using a different endianness, no additional SFENCE.VMA is necessary beyond what would ordinarily be required for the world switch.

RULE 14: An implementation supports only big-endian memory accesses aside from instruction fetches if the MBE field in mstatus or mstatush is read-only 1 and the SBE and UBE fields in mstatus or mstatush are each read-only 1 when S-mode and U-mode, respectively, are supported.

RULE 15: The correspondence between addresses and byte locations is fixed and is not affected by any endianness mode controlled by mstatus or mstatush.

RULE 16: The applicable endianness mode controlled by mstatus or mstatush determines the order of mapping between memory bytes and a multibyte quantity, such as a halfword or word.

RULE 17: RISC-V instructions are uniformly little-endian regardless of the endianness settings in mstatus and mstatush.

RULE 18: RISC-V instruction endianness is decoupled from the current endianness settings in mstatus and mstatush.

RULE 19: When mstatus or mstatush selects big-endian mode, software that encodes or decodes machine instructions must account for explicit loads and stores having endianness opposite that of instructions, for example by swapping byte order after loads and before stores.

The following description:

- The TVM (Trap Virtual Memory) bit in mstatus is a WARL field that supports intercepting supervisor virtual-memory management operations.

- When TVM=1, attempts to read or write the satp CSR while executing in S-mode will raise an illegal-instruction exception.

- When TVM=1, attempts to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction while executing in S-mode will raise an illegal-instruction exception.

- When TVM=1, attempts to execute a SINVAL.VMA instruction while executing in S-mode will raise an illegal-instruction exception.

- When TVM=0, reading or writing the satp CSR and executing SFENCE.VMA or SINVAL.VMA are permitted in S-mode.

- TVM is read-only 0 when S-mode is not supported.

- The TVM mechanism improves virtualization efficiency by permitting guest operating systems to execute in S-mode, rather than classically virtualizing them in U-mode. This approach obviates the need to trap accesses to most S-mode CSRs.

- Trapping satp accesses and the SFENCE.VMA and SINVAL.VMA instructions provides the hooks necessary to lazily populate shadow page tables.

- The TW (Timeout Wait) bit in mstatus is a WARL field that supports intercepting the WFI instruction.

- When TW=0, the WFI instruction may execute in modes less privileged than M when not prevented for some other reason.

- When TW=1, if WFI is executed in any less-privileged mode and it does not complete within an implementation-specific, bounded time limit, the WFI instruction causes an illegal-instruction exception.

- When TW=1, an implementation may have WFI always raise an illegal-instruction exception in modes less privileged than M, even if there are pending globally-disabled interrupts when the instruction is executed.

- TW is read-only 0 when there are no modes less privileged than M.

- Trapping the WFI instruction can trigger a world switch to another guest OS, rather than wastefully idling in the current guest.

- When S-mode is implemented, executing WFI in U-mode causes an illegal-instruction exception, regardless of the value of the TW bit, unless the instruction completes within an implementation-specific, bounded time limit.

- The TSR (Trap SRET) bit in mstatus is a WARL field that supports intercepting the supervisor exception return instruction, SRET.

- When TSR=1, attempts to execute SRET while executing in S-mode will raise an illegal-instruction exception.

- When TSR=0, executing SRET is permitted in S-mode.

- TSR is read-only 0 when S-mode is not supported.

- Trapping SRET is necessary to emulate the hypervisor extension on implementations that do not provide it.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================

RULE 5: When the TVM bit in mstatus is 1, an attempt to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction while executing in S-mode will raise an illegal-instruction exception.

RULE 6: When the TVM bit in mstatus is 1, an attempt to execute a SINVAL.VMA instruction while executing in S-mode will raise an illegal-instruction exception.

RULE 7: When the TVM bit in mstatus is 0, reading the satp CSR is permitted in S-mode.

RULE 8: When the TVM bit in mstatus is 0, writing the satp CSR is permitted in S-mode.

RULE 10: When the TVM bit in mstatus is 0, executing SINVAL.VMA is permitted in S-mode.

The following description:

### FS, VS, XS, and SD fields

- The FS[1:0] and VS[1:0] fields are WARL fields. The XS[1:0] field is read-only.

- The FS, VS, and XS fields are used to reduce the cost of context save and restore by setting and tracking the current state of the floating-point unit and other user-mode extensions.

- The FS field encodes the status of the floating-point unit state, including the floating-point registers f0-f31 and the CSRs fcsr, frm, and fflags.

- The VS field encodes the status of the vector extension state, including the vector registers v0-v31 and the CSRs vcsr, vxrm, vxsat, vstart, vl, vtype, and vlenb.

- The XS field encodes the status of additional user-mode extensions and associated state.

- The FS, VS, and XS fields can be checked by a context-switch routine to quickly determine whether a state save or restore is required. If a save or restore is required, additional instructions and CSRs are typically required to effect and optimize the process.

- The SD bit provides a fast check for whether state in either or both of the floating-point unit or other extensions needs to be saved or restored.

- The FS, VS, and XS fields use the following status encoding:

| Status | FS and VS meaning | XS meaning                    |
|--------|-------------------|-------------------------------|
| 0      | Off               | All off                       |
| 1      | Initial           | None dirty or clean, some on  |
| 2      | Clean             | None dirty, some clean        |
| 3      | Dirty             | Some dirty                    |

### Conditions affecting whether FS is read-only zero

- If the F extension is implemented, the FS field shall not be read-only zero.

- If neither the F extension nor S-mode is implemented, FS is read-only zero.

- If S-mode is implemented but the F extension is not, FS may optionally be read-only zero.

- Implementations with S-mode but without the F extension are permitted, but not required, to make the FS field read-only zero.

- Some implementations with S-mode but without the F extension will choose not to have the FS field be read-only zero, so as to enable emulation of the F extension for both S-mode and U-mode via invisible traps into M-mode.

### Conditions affecting whether VS is read-only zero

- If the v registers are implemented, the VS field shall not be read-only zero.

- If neither the v registers nor S-mode is implemented, VS is read-only zero.

- If S-mode is implemented but the v registers are not, VS may optionally be read-only zero.

### XS summary behavior

- In harts without additional user extensions requiring new state, the XS field is read-only zero.

- Every additional extension with state provides a CSR field that encodes the equivalent of the XS states.

- The XS field represents a summary of all extensions' status according to the XS encoding.

- The XS field effectively reports the maximum status value across all user-extension status fields, though individual extensions can use a different encoding than XS.

- The XS field is set indirectly by writing to the status register of the individual extensions.

- The XS field provides a summary of all added extension state, but additional microarchitectural bits might be maintained in an extension to further reduce context-save and restore overhead.

### SD behavior

- The SD bit is read-only.

- The SD bit summarizes whether either the FS, VS, or XS fields signal the presence of some dirty state that will require saving extended user context to memory.

- If FS, XS, and VS are all read-only zero, SD is also always zero.

- The SD bit is set when either the FS, VS, or XS bits encode a Dirty state:

  SD = (FS == 0b11 OR XS == 0b11 OR VS == 0b11)

- The SD bit allows privileged code to quickly determine when no additional context save is required beyond the integer register set and pc.

### Meaning and architectural effect of each status

- When an extension's status is set to Off, any instruction that attempts to read or write the corresponding state will cause an illegal-instruction exception.

- When the status is Initial, the corresponding state should have an initial constant value.

- When the status is Clean, the corresponding state is potentially different from the initial value, but matches the last value stored on a context swap.

- When the status is Dirty, the corresponding state has potentially been modified since the last context save.

### Context-save and context-restore behavior

- During a context save, the responsible privileged code need only write out the corresponding state if its status is Dirty, and can then reset the extension's status to Clean.

- During a context restore, the context need only be loaded from memory if the status is Clean. The status should never be Dirty at restore.

- If the status is Initial, the context must be set to an initial constant value on context restore to avoid a security hole, but this can be done without accessing memory. For example, the floating-point registers can all be initialized to the immediate value 0.

- The FS and XS fields are read by privileged code before saving the context.

- The FS field is set directly by privileged code when resuming a user context.

- The status fields will also be updated during execution of instructions, regardless of privilege mode.

### FS, VS, and XS state transitions

The following are all possible state transitions for the FS, VS, or XS status bits.

#### At context save in privileged code

- If the current state is Off, state is not saved and the next state is Off.

- If the current state is Initial, state is not saved and the next state is Initial.

- If the current state is Clean, state is not saved and the next state is Clean.

- If the current state is Dirty, state is saved and the next state is Clean.

#### At context restore in privileged code

- If the current state is Off, state is not restored and the next state is Off.

- If the current state is Initial, state is restored to initial and the next state is Initial.

- If the current state is Clean, state is restored from memory and the next state is Clean.

- If the current state is Dirty, the restore action and next state are N/A.

#### Execute instruction to read state

- If the current state is Off, the action is Exception and the next state is Off.

- If the current state is Initial, the action is Execute and the next state is Initial.

- If the current state is Clean, the action is Execute and the next state is Clean.

- If the current state is Dirty, the action is Execute and the next state is Dirty.

#### Execute instruction that possibly modifies state, including configuration

- If the current state is Off, the action is Exception and the next state is Off.

- If the current state is Initial, the action is Execute and the next state is Dirty.

- If the current state is Clean, the action is Execute and the next state is Dirty.

- If the current state is Dirty, the action is Execute and the next state is Dirty.

#### Execute instruction to unconfigure unit

- If the current state is Off, the action is Exception and the next state is Off.

- If the current state is Initial, the action is Execute and the next state is Initial.

- If the current state is Clean, the action is Execute and the next state is Initial.

- If the current state is Dirty, the action is Execute and the next state is Initial.

#### Execute instruction to disable unit

- If the current state is Off, the action is Execute and the next state is Off.

- If the current state is Initial, the action is Execute and the next state is Off.

- If the current state is Clean, the action is Execute and the next state is Off.

- If the current state is Dirty, the action is Execute and the next state is Off.

#### Execute instruction to enable unit

- If the current state is Off, the action is Execute and the next state is Initial.

- If the current state is Initial, the action is Execute and the next state is Initial.

- If the current state is Clean, the action is Execute and the next state is Initial.

- If the current state is Dirty, the action is Execute and the next state is Initial.

- The standard floating-point and vector extensions do not support user-mode unconfigure or disable/enable instructions.

### User-mode control of extension status

- A user-mode extension can define additional instructions to allow user-mode software to return the unit to an initial state or to turn off the unit.

- An unconfigured state can be represented as the Initial state for context save.

- If the same application remains running between an unconfigure and the next configure, which would set the status to Dirty, there is no need to actually reinitialize the state at the unconfigure instruction because all state is local to the user process. The Initial state may cause the state to be initialized to a constant value at context restore, not at every unconfigure.

- Executing a user-mode instruction to disable a unit and place it into the Off state will cause an illegal-instruction exception to be raised if any subsequent instruction tries to use the unit before it is turned back on.

- A user-mode instruction to turn a unit on must also ensure that the unit's state is properly initialized, as the unit might have been used by another context in the meantime.

### Effect of changing FS or VS

- Changing the setting of FS has no effect on the contents of the floating-point register state.

- Setting FS=Off does not destroy the floating-point register state.

- Setting FS=Initial does not clear the contents of the floating-point register state.

- Changing the setting of VS has no effect on the contents of the vector register state.

- Other extensions might not preserve state when set to Off.

### Imprecise FS dirtiness tracking

- Implementations may choose to track the dirtiness of the floating-point register state imprecisely by reporting the state to be Dirty even when it has not been modified.

- On some implementations, some instructions that do not mutate the floating-point state may cause the state to transition from Initial or Clean to Dirty.

- On implementations where floating-point dirtiness is not tracked at all, the valid FS states are Off and Dirty, and an attempt to set FS to Initial or Clean causes it to be set to Dirty.

- The definition of FS does not disallow setting FS to Dirty as a result of errant speculation.

- Some platforms may choose to disallow speculatively writing FS to close a potential side channel.

- If an instruction explicitly or implicitly writes a floating-point register or fcsr but does not alter its contents, and FS=Initial or FS=Clean, it is implementation-defined whether FS transitions to Dirty.

### Imprecise VS dirtiness tracking

- Implementations may choose to track the dirtiness of the vector register state in an analogous imprecise fashion, including possibly setting VS to Dirty when software attempts to set VS=Initial or VS=Clean.

- When VS=Initial or VS=Clean, it is implementation-defined whether an instruction that writes a vector register or vector CSR but does not alter its contents causes VS to transition to Dirty.

### Sharing across privilege modes

- Machine and Supervisor modes share a single copy of the FS, VS, and XS bits.

- Supervisor-level software normally uses the FS, VS, and XS bits directly to record the status with respect to the supervisor-level saved context.

- Machine-level software must be more conservative in saving and restoring extension state in its corresponding version of the context.

### Floating-point context handling related to FS

- The floating-point unit state is always initialized, saved, and restored using standard instructions from F, D, and/or Q.

- Privileged code must be aware of FLEN to determine the appropriate space to reserve for each f register.

The following description:

- An interrupt i will trap to M-mode, causing the privilege mode to change to M-mode, if all of the following are true:
  (a) either the current privilege mode is M and the MIE bit in the mstatus register is set, or the current privilege mode has less privilege than M-mode;
  (b) bit i is set in both mip and mie; and
  (c) if register mideleg exists, bit i is not set in mideleg.

- These conditions for an interrupt trap to occur must be evaluated in a bounded amount of time from when an interrupt becomes, or ceases to be, pending in mip.

- These conditions for an interrupt trap to occur must also be evaluated immediately following the execution of an xRET instruction or an explicit write to a CSR on which these interrupt trap conditions expressly depend, including mip, mie, mstatus, and mideleg.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: An interrupt i will trap to M-mode and cause the privilege mode to change to M-mode if either the current privilege mode is M-mode and the MIE bit in mstatus is set or the current privilege mode has less privilege than M-mode, bit i is set in both mip and mie, and, if mideleg exists, bit i is not set in mideleg.

RULE 2: The interrupt-trap conditions that depend on the MIE bit in mstatus must be evaluated within a bounded amount of time after an interrupt becomes pending or ceases to be pending in mip.

RULE 3: The interrupt-trap conditions that depend on the MIE bit in mstatus must be evaluated immediately following execution of an xRET instruction.

RULE 4: The interrupt-trap conditions that expressly depend on mstatus must be evaluated immediately following an explicit write to mstatus.

The following description:

The following description:

One way to prevent the use of new user-level CSRs as covert channels would be to add to mstatus or sstatus an "XS" field for each relevant extension, paralleling the V extension's VS field. However, this is not considered a general solution to the problem due to the number of potential future extensions that may add small amounts of state.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: Adding an XS field to mstatus for each relevant extension, paralleling the V extension's VS field, would be one way to prevent new user-level CSRs from being used as covert channels.
RULE 2: Adding an XS field to mstatus for each relevant extension is not considered a general solution to covert channels because many potential future extensions may add small amounts of state.

The following description:

- An attempt to execute SINVAL.VMA or HINVAL.GVMA in S-mode or HS-mode when mstatus.TVM=1 raises an illegal-instruction exception.

- SFENCE.W.INVAL and SFENCE.INVAL.IR are unaffected by the mstatus.TVM field and hence are always permitted in S-mode and VS-mode.

- SFENCE.W.INVAL and SFENCE.INVAL.IR do not need to be trapped when mstatus.TVM=1, as they only have ordering effects but no visible side effects. Trapping of the SINVAL.VMA instruction is sufficient to enable emulation of the intended overall TLB maintenance functionality.

=========
